<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\cite{BJK}\label{defact}
Let $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ be a semi-abelian category and let $B$ be an object of~$\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$. An \emph{internal action} of $B$ on an object $X$ of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ is a morphism $\xi\colon B\flat X\to X$ such that $(X,\xi)$ is an algebra over the monad $B\flat(-)$, that is, such that the diagrams
\begin{equation*}
\begin{tikzcd}[baseline=(current bounding box.center)]
X
    \arrow[r, "\eta^{B}_{X}"]
    \arrow[dr, equal]
&
B\flat X
    \arrow[d, "\xi"]
\\
&
X
\end{tikzcd}
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\begin{tikzcd}[baseline=(current bounding box.center)]
B\flat(B\flat X)
    \arrow[r, "\mu^{B}_{X}"]
    \arrow[d, "\operatorname{id}_B\flat\xi"']
&
B\flat X
    \arrow[d, "\xi"]
\\
B\flat X
    \arrow[r, "\xi"']
&
X
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation*}
commute.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\cite{BJK}\label{actspltnatural}
For every object $X$ of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$, there is a natural isomorphism of
functors
\[
\operatorname{Act}(-,X)\cong\operatorname{SplExt}(-,X).
\]
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\cite{BJK}
A semi-abelian category $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ is said to be \emph{action representable}
if, for every object $X$ of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$, the functor $\operatorname{SplExt}(-,X)$ is
representable. In other words, there exists an object $[X]$ of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$,
called the \emph{actor} of $X$, together with a natural isomorphism of
functors
\begin{align*}
\operatorname{SplExt}(-,X)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}}(-,[X]).
\end{align*}
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
Recall that an (external) action of a group $B$ on a group $X$ is a
map
\[
\xi\colon B\times X\to X\colon (b,x)\mapsto b\cdot x
\]
such that $1_B\cdot x=x$, $(bb')\cdot x=b\cdot(b'\cdot x)$, and
$b\cdot(xx')=(b\cdot x)(b\cdot x')$, for all $b,b'\in B$ and
$x,x'\in X$. Here, we use multiplicative notation for both $B$ and
$X$.

Given an action $\xi\colon B\times X\to X$ in $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Grp}}$, one can
construct the \emph{semidirect product} $B\ltimes X$ of $B$ and
$X$ with respect to $\xi$. Its underlying set is the Cartesian product
$B\times X$, and its multiplication is given by
\[
(b,x)(b',x')=(bb',x(b\cdot x')),
\]
with $1_{B\ltimes X}=(1_B,1_X)$ and
$(b,x)^{-1}=(b^{-1},b^{-1}\cdot x^{-1})$. Thus, we can associate with~$\xi$ the following split extension in $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Grp}}$:
\begin{equation}\label{splextgrpsemidirect}
\begin{tikzcd}
X \arrow[r, "\iota_2"]
&
B\ltimes X \arrow[r, shift left, "\pi_1"]
&
B \arrow[l, shift left, "\iota_1"],
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
where $\iota_2(x)=(1_B,x)$, $\iota_1(b)=(b,1_X)$, and $\pi_1(b,x)=b$.

Conversely, suppose that we start with a split extension in $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Grp}}$
\begin{equation}\label{splextgrp}
\begin{tikzcd}
X \arrow[r, "k"]
&
A \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha"]
&
B \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta"].
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
We can then define an action of $B$ on $X$ by\footnote{Indeed, one has
\[
\alpha\bigl(\beta(b)k(x)\beta(b)^{-1}\bigr)
=\alpha(\beta(b))\alpha(k(x))\alpha(\beta(b))^{-1}
=b1_Bb^{-1}=1_B.
\]
Hence, $\beta(b)k(x)\beta(b)^{-1}\in\ker(\alpha)\cong X$, so that
$k^{-1}\bigl(\beta(b)k(x)\beta(b)^{-1}\bigr)$ is well defined.}
\begin{equation}\label{actgrp}
B\times X\to X\colon
(b,x)\mapsto
k^{-1}\bigl(\beta(b)k(x)\beta(b)^{-1}\bigr)
\end{equation}
and there is an isomorphism of split extensions
\begin{equation*}
\begin{tikzcd}
X
    \arrow[r, "\iota_2"]
    \arrow[d, equal]
&
B\ltimes X
    \arrow[r, shift left, "\pi_1"]
    \arrow[d, "\theta"']
&
B
    \arrow[l, shift left, "\iota_1"]
    \arrow[d, equal]
\\
X \arrow[r, "k"]
&
A \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha"]
&
B \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta"],
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation*}
where $B\ltimes X$ is the semidirect product associated with
\eqref{actgrp} and $\theta(b,x)=k(x)\beta(b)$.

Moreover, isomorphism classes of split extensions of groups are in bijection with group homomorphisms $B\to\operatorname{Aut}(X)$, where $\operatorname{Aut}(X)$ denotes the group of \emph{automorphisms} of $X$. Given a split extension of groups as in \eqref{splextgrp}, one can define a group homomorphism
\[
\varphi\colon B\to\operatorname{Aut}(X)\colon b\mapsto\varphi_b,
\]
where
$\varphi_b(x)=k^{-1}\bigl(\beta(b)k(x)\beta(b)^{-1}\bigr)$.
Conversely, every morphism $\varphi\colon B\to\operatorname{Aut}(X)$ defines a
semidirect product $B\ltimes X$ with multiplication
\[
(b,x)(b',x')=(bb',x\varphi_b(x')),
\]
and hence a split extension of $B$ by $X$ as in
\eqref{splextgrpsemidirect}.

This bijection is natural in $B$, since the diagram in $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Set}}$
\[
\begin{tikzcd}
\operatorname{SplExt}(B,X)
    \arrow[r]
    \arrow[d, swap, "{f^*}"]
&
\operatorname{Hom}_\ensuremath{\mathsf{Grp}}(B,\operatorname{Aut}(X))
    \arrow[d, "{-\circ f}"]
\\
\operatorname{SplExt}(B',X)
    \arrow[r]
&
\operatorname{Hom}_\ensuremath{\mathsf{Grp}}(B',\operatorname{Aut}(X))
\end{tikzcd}
\]
commutes for every group homomorphism $f\colon B'\to B$. Thus,
$\operatorname{Aut}(X)$ is the actor of $X$, and the category $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Grp}}$ is action
representable.
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- example-2: body_md -->
\label{exliealgebras}
The same argument applies to the category of Lie algebras over a field,
with $\operatorname{Aut}(X)$ replaced by the Lie algebra of derivations $\operatorname{Der}(X)$.

Let $\mathbb{F}$ be a field. Recall that a Lie algebra $(X,[-,-])$ is a
vector space $X$ over $\mathbb{F}$ endowed with a bilinear map
\[
[-,-]\colon X\times X\to X,
\]
called \emph{commutator} or \emph{Lie bracket}, which is
\emph{alternating}, that is, $[x,x]=0$ for any~$x\in X$, and
satisfies the \emph{Jacobi identity}
\[
[x,[y,z]]+[y,[z,x]]+[z,[x,y]]=0
\]
for all $x,y,z\in X$. We denote by $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Lie}}$ the category of Lie algebras
over $\mathbb{F}$, whose morphisms are the $\mathbb{F}$-linear maps preserving the
bracket.

An (external) action of a Lie algebra $B$ on a Lie algebra $X$ is
given by a pair of bilinear maps
\[
l\colon B\times X\to X,
\qquad
r\colon X\times B\to X,
\]
denoted by $[b,x]=l(b,x)$ and $[x,b]=r(x,b)$, such that
\begin{itemize}
\item[(i)] $[x,b]=-[b,x]$;
\item[(ii)]
$[b,[x,x']]=[[b,x],x']+[x,[b,x']]$;
\item[(iii)]
$[[b,b'],x]=[b,[b',x]]
-[b',[b,x]]$;
\end{itemize}
for all $b,b'\in B$ and $x,x'\in X$. The corresponding semidirect
product $B\ltimes X$ is the Lie algebra defined on the direct sum of
vector spaces $B\oplus X$ with commutator
\[
[(b,x),(b',x')]
=
([b,b'],[x,x']+[b,x']-[b',x]).
\]
Thus, we can associate with the action $(l,r)$ the following split
extension in $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Lie}}$:
\begin{equation}\label{splextliesemidirect}
\begin{tikzcd}
X \arrow[r, "\iota_2"]
&
B\ltimes X \arrow[r, shift left, "\pi_1"]
&
B. \arrow[l, shift left, "\iota_1"]
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
Here, $B\ltimes X=(B\oplus X,[-,-])$ is the semidirect product of $B$ and $X$ with respect to the bilinear maps $(l,r)$, while $\iota_2(x)=(0,x)$, $\iota_1(b)=(b,0)$, and
$\pi_1(b,x)=b$.

Conversely, suppose that we start with a split extension in $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Lie}}$
\begin{equation}\label{splextlie}
\begin{tikzcd}
X \arrow[r, "k"]
&
A \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha"]
&
B. \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta"]
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
We can then define an action of $B$ on $X$ by\footnote{Again, one has
\[
\alpha\bigl([\beta(b),k(x)]\bigr)
=[\alpha(\beta(b)),\alpha(k(x))]
=[b,0]=0.
\]
Hence, $[\beta(b),k(x)]\in\ker(\alpha) \cong X$, so that
$k^{-1}\bigl([\beta(b),k(x)]\bigr)$ is well defined.}
\begin{equation}\label{actlie}
[b,x]=-[x,b]=k^{-1}\bigl([\beta(b),k(x)]\bigr),
\end{equation}
and there is an isomorphism of split extensions
\begin{equation*}
\begin{tikzcd}
X
    \arrow[r, "\iota_2"]
    \arrow[d, equal]
&
B\ltimes X
    \arrow[r, shift left, "\pi_1"]
    \arrow[d, "\theta"']
&
B
    \arrow[l, shift left, "\iota_1"]
    \arrow[d, equal]
\\
X \arrow[r, "k"]
&
A \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha"]
&
B \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta"].
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation*}
Here, $B\ltimes X$ is the semidirect product associated with
\eqref{actlie} and $\theta(b,x)=\beta(b)+k(x)$.

We further recall that a derivation of a Lie algebra $X$ is a linear
endomorphism $d\colon X\to X$ such that
\[
d([x,y])=[d(x),y]+[x,d(y)],
\]
for all $x,y\in X$. The vector space
$\operatorname{Der}(X)$ of derivations of $X$ is a Lie algebra with respect to the
bracket
\[
[d,d']=d\circ d'-d'\circ d.
\]
The isomorphism classes of split extensions of Lie algebras are in bijection with the Lie algebra homomorphisms $B\to\operatorname{Der}(X)$. Given a split extension of Lie algebras as in~\eqref{splextlie}, one can define a Lie algebra homomorphism
\[
\varphi\colon B\to\operatorname{Der}(X)\colon b\mapsto\varphi_b,
\]
where $\varphi_b(x)=k^{-1}\bigl([\beta(b),k(x)]_A\bigr)$.
Conversely, every morphism $\varphi\colon B\to\operatorname{Der}(X)$ defines a
semidirect product $B\ltimes X$ with Lie bracket
\[
[(b,x),(b',x')]
=
([b,b'],[x,x']+\varphi_b(x')-\varphi_{b'}(x)),
\]
and hence a split extension of $B$ by $X$ as in
\eqref{splextliesemidirect}.

This bijection is natural in $B$, since the diagram in $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Set}}$
\[
\begin{tikzcd}
\operatorname{SplExt}(B,X)
    \arrow[r]
    \arrow[d, swap, "{f^*}"]
&
\operatorname{Hom}_\ensuremath{\mathsf{Lie}}(B,\operatorname{Der}(X))
    \arrow[d, "{-\circ f}"]
\\
\operatorname{SplExt}(B',X)
    \arrow[r]
&
\operatorname{Hom}_\ensuremath{\mathsf{Lie}}(B',\operatorname{Der}(X))
\end{tikzcd}
\]
commutes for every Lie algebra homomorphism $f\colon B'\to B$. Thus,
$\operatorname{Der}(X)$ is the actor of $X$, and the category $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Lie}}$ is action
representable.
<!-- end example-2 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
Let $\ensuremath{\mathsf{SplExt}(X)}$ be the category of split extensions in $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ with fixed kernel~$X$, whose morphisms are triples
$(\operatorname{id}_X,\theta,g)$ making the diagrams of split extensions commute. Since a functor $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}^{\operatorname{op}}\to\ensuremath{\mathsf{Set}}$ is representable if and only if its
category of elements has a terminal object (see~\cite{BJK}), action
representability is equivalent to requiring that, for every object
$X$ of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$, the category $\ensuremath{\mathsf{SplExt}(X)}$ has a terminal object of the form
\begin{equation*}
\begin{tikzcd}
X \arrow[r, "k_X"]
&
{[X]\ltimes X} \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha_X"]
&
{[X]} \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta_X"].
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation*}
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\cite{BJK}\label{termobj}
A semi-abelian category $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ is action representable if and only if,
for every object $X$ of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$, there exist an object $[X]$ of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$
and an internal action of~$[X]$ on $X$ with the following universal
property: for every object $B$ of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ and every split extension
\begin{equation*}
\begin{tikzcd}
X \arrow[r, "k"]
&
A \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha"]
&
B \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta"],
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation*}
there is a unique morphism $b \colon B\to[X]$ such that there exists a morphism
$\theta \colon A\to[X]\ltimes X$ making the following diagram commute:
\begin{equation*} 
\begin{tikzcd}
X
    \arrow[r, "k"]
    \arrow[d, equal]
&
A
    \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha"]
    \arrow[d, "\theta"']
&
B
    \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta"]
    \arrow[d, "b"]
\\
X \arrow[r, "k_X"]
&
{[X]\ltimes X} \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha_X"]
&
{[X]} \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta_X"].
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation*}
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Suppose first that $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ is action representable. Let $X$ be an
object of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$, and let $[X]$ be its actor. The identity morphism
$\operatorname{id}_{[X]}$ determines an internal action $\xi$ of~$[X]$ on $X$.
Denote by
\begin{equation*}
\begin{tikzcd}
X \arrow[r, "k_X"]
&
{[X]\ltimes X} \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha_X"]
&
{[X]} \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta_X"]
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation*}
the split extension associated with $\xi$ under the natural bijection
of \Cref{actspltnatural}.

The category of elements $\mathsf{el}(\operatorname{SplExt}(-,X))$ has a terminal
object given by this split extension. Thus, for every object
$B$ of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ and every split extension
\begin{equation*}
\begin{tikzcd}
X \arrow[r, "k"]
&
A \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha"]
&
B \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta"],
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation*}
there exist a unique morphism $b\colon B\to[X]$ and a (necessarily unique) morphism $\theta$ such that the
following diagram commutes:
\begin{equation*}
\begin{tikzcd}
X
    \arrow[r, "k"]
    \arrow[d, equal]
&
A
    \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha"]
    \arrow[d, "\theta"']
&
B
    \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta"]
    \arrow[d, "b"]
\\
X \arrow[r, "k_X"]
&
{[X]\ltimes X} \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha_X"]
&
{[X]} \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta_X"].
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation*}
The morphism $\theta$ is uniquely determined by $\operatorname{id}_X$ and $b$, and the right-hand square is a pullback.

Conversely, suppose that, for every object $X$ of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$, there exists
an object $[X]$ acting on $X$ and satisfying the above universal
property. For every object $B$ of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$, let
\[
\tau_B\colon\operatorname{SplExt}(B,X)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}}(B,[X])
\]
send the isomorphism class of a split extension to the unique morphism
provided by the universal property. On the other hand, every morphism
$b\colon B\to[X]$ determines a split extension of $B$ by $X$ by
pulling back along $b$ the split extension associated with the action of $[X]$ on $X$. These two assignments are mutually inverse: one composite is the identity by the uniqueness in the universal property, and the other one because, by protomodularity, the right-hand square of a morphism of split extensions which is the identity on kernels is a pullback. This establishes a bijection
\[
\operatorname{SplExt}(B,X)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}}(B,[X])
\]
for every object $B$ of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$, and naturality follows from the
uniqueness in the universal property.
Hence, the maps~$\tau_B$ determine a natural isomorphism
\[
\operatorname{SplExt}(-,X)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}}(-,[X]).
\]
Therefore, $[X]$ is the actor of $X$, and $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ is action
representable.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- example-3: body_md -->
Let $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}=\ensuremath{\mathsf{Grp}}$, and let $X$ be a group. Then $\operatorname{Aut}(X)$ acts on $X$ via
\[
\operatorname{Aut}(X)\times X\to X\colon(\varphi,x)\mapsto\varphi(x).
\]
The corresponding semidirect product $\operatorname{Aut}(X)\ltimes X$ is called the
\emph{holomorph} of $X$ and is denoted by $\operatorname{Hol}(X)$;
see~\cite{Burnside}. Its multiplication is given by
\[
(\varphi,x)(\varphi',x')
=
(\varphi\circ\varphi',x\varphi(x')).
\]
<!-- end example-3 -->
<!-- example-4: body_md -->
\label{exactreplie}
Let $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}=\ensuremath{\mathsf{Lie}}$, and let $X$ be a Lie algebra. The action of the Lie
algebra $\operatorname{Der}(X)$ on $X$ is defined by the pair of bilinear maps
\[
\operatorname{Der}(X)\times X\to X\colon(d,x)\mapsto d(x)
\]
and
\[
X\times\operatorname{Der}(X)\to X\colon(x,d)\mapsto-d(x).
\]
The associated semidirect product $\operatorname{Der}(X)\ltimes X$ is again called
the \emph{holomorph} of $X$ and is denoted by
$\operatorname{Hol}(X)$; see~\cite{jacobsonLie}. Its Lie bracket is
given by
\[
[(d,x),(d',x')]
=
(d\circ d'-d'\circ d,[x,x']+d(x')-d'(x)).
\]
<!-- end example-4 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\cite{act_accessible}\label{defactacc}
A semi-abelian category $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ is \emph{action accessible} if, for every
object $X$ of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$, every object of $\ensuremath{\mathsf{SplExt}(X)}$ is accessible.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
By definition, the existence of a terminal object in $\ensuremath{\mathsf{SplExt}(X)}$ is
stronger than the accessibility of each of its objects. It follows
immediately that
\[
\textit{action representability}
\Rightarrow
\textit{action accessibility}.
\]
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\cite{WRA}\label{defWRA}
A semi-abelian category $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ is said to be \emph{weakly action
representable} if, for every object $X$ of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$, there exist an object
$T=T(X)$ of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ and a natural monomorphism of functors
\[
\tau\colon\operatorname{SplExt}(-,X)\rightarrowtail\operatorname{Hom}_{\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}}(-,T).
\]
The pair $(T,\tau)$ is called a \emph{weak representation} of
$\operatorname{SplExt}(-,X)$, the object $T$ is called a \emph{weak representing
object} of $X$, and a morphism $\varphi\colon B\to T$ belonging to
the image of $\tau_B$ is called an \emph{acting morphism}.
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
It follows from \Cref{defWRA} that a weak representing object need not
be unique. Indeed, if $(T,\tau)$ is a weak representation of
$\operatorname{SplExt}(-,X)$ and $i\colon T\rightarrowtail T'$ is a monomorphism in
$\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$, then $i$ induces a natural monomorphism of functors
\[
i_*\colon
\operatorname{Hom}_{\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}}(-,T)
\rightarrowtail
\operatorname{Hom}_{\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}}(-,T'),
\]
and $(T',i_*\circ\tau)$ is another weak representation of
$\operatorname{SplExt}(-,X)$.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- example-5: body_md -->
Every action representable category is weakly action representable. In
this case, $T=[X]$ is the actor of $X$, the natural monomorphism
$\tau$ is a natural isomorphism, and every morphism
$\varphi\colon B\to[X]$ is an acting morphism.
<!-- end example-5 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
\label{exassoc}
Consider a split extension in $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Assoc}}$
\begin{equation}\label{diag:SplAssoc}
\begin{tikzcd}
X \arrow[r, "k"]
&
A \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha"]
&
B \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta"].
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
We can define a pair of bilinear maps
\[
l\colon B\times X\to X,
\qquad
r\colon X\times B\to X
\]
by
\[
b\ast x
=
l(b,x)
=
k^{-1}\bigl(\beta(b)k(x)\bigr),
\qquad
x\ast b
=
r(x,b)
=
k^{-1}\bigl(k(x)\beta(b)\bigr),
\]
for all $b\in B$ and $x\in X$\footnote{These expressions are well defined, since $\beta(b)k(x),k(x)\beta(b)\in\ker(\alpha)$, and the corestriction $k \colon X \to \ker(\alpha)$ is an isomorphism.}. Moreover, we can endow the direct sum
of vector spaces~$B\oplus X$ with the bilinear multiplication
\begin{equation}\label{product on semi direct assoc}
(b,x)\cdot(b',x')
=
(bb',xx'+b\ast x'+x\ast b').
\end{equation}
Then $(B\oplus X,\cdot)$ is an associative algebra, called the
\emph{semidirect product} of $B$ and $X$ with respect to $(l,r)$ and
denoted by $B\ltimes X$. The diagram
\begin{equation*}
\begin{tikzcd}
X \arrow[r, "\iota_2"]
&
B\ltimes X \arrow[r, shift left, "\pi_1"]
&
B \arrow[l, shift left, "\iota_1"]
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation*}
with $\iota_2(x)=(0,x)$, $\iota_1(b)=(b,0)$, and
$\pi_1(b,x)=b$, defines a split extension in $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Assoc}}$ which is
isomorphic to~\eqref{diag:SplAssoc} via
\begin{equation*}
\begin{tikzcd}
X
    \arrow[r, "\iota_2"]
    \arrow[d, equal]
&
B\ltimes X
    \arrow[r, shift left, "\pi_1"]
    \arrow[d, "\theta"']
&
B
    \arrow[l, shift left, "\iota_1"]
    \arrow[d, equal]
\\
X \arrow[r, "k"]
&
A \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha"]
&
B \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta"].
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation*}
Here, $\theta(b,x)=\beta(b)+k(x)$ for every $(b,x)\in B\oplus X$.

The associativity of the multiplication
in~\eqref{product on semi direct assoc} implies the following
identities:
\begin{itemize}
\item[(i)] $b\ast(xx')=(b\ast x)x'$;
\item[(ii)] $(xx')\ast b=x(x'\ast b)$;
\item[(iii)] $x(b\ast x')=(x\ast b)x'$;
\item[(iv)] $(bb')\ast x=b\ast(b'\ast x)$;
\item[(v)] $x\ast(bb')=(x\ast b)\ast b'$;
\item[(vi)] $b\ast(x\ast b')=(b\ast x)\ast b'$;
\end{itemize}
for all $b,b'\in B$ and $x,x'\in X$. The first three identities state
that the pair $(b\ast-,-\ast b)$ belongs to the associative algebra
\[
\operatorname{Bim}(X)
=
\left\{
(f\ast-,-\ast f)
\in
\operatorname{End}(X)\times\operatorname{End}(X)^{\operatorname{op}}
\,\middle|\,
\begin{aligned}
f\ast(xy) &= (f\ast x)y,\\
(xy)\ast f &= x(y\ast f),\\
x(f\ast y) &= (x\ast f)y
\end{aligned}
\, \forall x,y\in X
\right\}
\]
of \emph{bimultipliers}, or \emph{bimultiplications}, of
$X$~\cite{MacLane58}. Its bilinear multiplication is given by
\[
(f\ast-,-\ast f)\cdot(g\ast-,-\ast g)
=
\bigl(f\ast(g\ast-),(-\ast f)\ast g\bigr).
\]
Identities~(iv) and~(v) imply that the linear map
\[
B\to\operatorname{Bim}(X)\colon b\mapsto(b\ast-,-\ast b)
\]
is a homomorphism of associative algebras. Moreover, this homomorphism
satisfies~(vi), which states that the left multiplier $b\ast-$ and
the right multiplier $-\ast b'$ are permutable.

Conversely, let
\[
\varphi\colon B\to\operatorname{Bim}(X)\colon
b\mapsto(b\ast_{\varphi}-,-\ast_{\varphi}b)
\]
be an associative algebra homomorphism satisfying
$b\ast_{\varphi}(x\ast_{\varphi}b')
=(b\ast_{\varphi}x)\ast_{\varphi}b'$. We can associate with
$\varphi$ the split extension in $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Assoc}}$
\begin{equation*}
\begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r]
&
X \arrow[r, "\iota_2"]
&
{(B\oplus X,\cdot_{\varphi})}
    \arrow[r, shift left, "\pi_1"]
&
B \arrow[l, shift left, "\iota_1"] \arrow[r]
&
0,
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation*}
where the associative algebra structure on $B\oplus X$ is given by
\[
(b,x)\cdot_{\varphi}(b',x')
=
(bb',xx'+b\ast_{\varphi}x'+x\ast_{\varphi}b').
\]
As a consequence, we obtain the following result.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thmassoc}\cite{CigoliManciniMetere, WRA}
Let $B$ and $X$ be associative algebras over $\mathbb{F}$.
\begin{itemize}
\item[(1)] The set of isomorphism classes of split extensions of $B$
by $X$ is in bijection with the set of associative algebra
homomorphisms
\[
B\to\operatorname{Bim}(X)\colon b\mapsto(b\ast-,-\ast b)
\]
satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{condition_assoc}
b\ast(x\ast b')=(b\ast x)\ast b'
\end{equation}
for all $b,b'\in B$ and $x\in X$.

\item[(2)] The category $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Assoc}}$ of associative algebras over $\mathbb{F}$
is weakly action representable, and $\operatorname{Bim}(X)$
is a weak representing object of $X$.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
\label{exnotacting}
A general homomorphism $B\to\operatorname{Bim}(X)$ need not give rise to a split
extension in $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Assoc}}$. For instance, let $B=\mathbb{F}\{b\}$ and
$X=\mathbb{F}\{e_1,e_2\}$ be abelian algebras, where $\mathbb{F}\{S\}$ denotes the vector space with basis~$S$. Then
\[
\operatorname{Bim}(X)=\operatorname{End}(X)\times\operatorname{End}(X)^{\operatorname{op}},
\]
and the homomorphism
$\varphi\colon B\to\operatorname{Bim}(X) \colon b \mapsto (b \ast_\varphi -, - \ast_\varphi b)$ defined by
\[
b\ast_{\varphi}e_1=e_2,
\quad
b\ast_{\varphi}e_2=0,
\qquad
e_1\ast_{\varphi}b=0,
\quad
e_2\ast_{\varphi}b=e_1
\]
does not satisfy~(vi), since
\[
(b\ast_{\varphi}e_1)\ast_{\varphi}b
=
e_2\ast_{\varphi}b
=
e_1
\neq
0
=
b\ast_{\varphi}0
=
b\ast_{\varphi}(e_1\ast_{\varphi}b).
\]
Condition~\eqref{condition_assoc} can nevertheless be used to
characterise the acting morphisms in $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Assoc}}$. In~\cite{MacLane58},
S.~Mac Lane described, for a ring $\Lambda$, the
$\Lambda$-bimodule structures on an abelian group $K$ in terms of ring
homomorphisms from $\Lambda$ to the ring of bimultipliers of $K$. The
following result is a straightforward generalisation to actions on an
object that is not necessarily abelian.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\cite{CigoliManciniMetere}\label{actmorpAssoc}
Let $B$ and $X$ be associative algebras over $\mathbb{F}$, and let
\[
\varphi\colon B\to\operatorname{Bim}(X)\colon
b\mapsto(b\ast_{\varphi}-,-\ast_{\varphi}b)
\]
be a morphism in $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Assoc}}$. Then $\varphi$ is an \emph{acting morphism}
if and only if
\begin{equation}\label{eqAssoc2}
b\ast_{\varphi}(x\ast_{\varphi}b')
=
(b\ast_{\varphi}x)\ast_{\varphi}b'
\end{equation}
for all $b,b'\in B$ and $x\in X$.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
\cite{WRA}
Let $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ be a weakly action representable category. It follows from
\Cref{defWRA} that, given two objects of $\ensuremath{\mathsf{SplExt}(X)}$ and a morphism
between them
\begin{equation*}
\begin{tikzcd}
X
    \arrow[r, "k"]
    \arrow[d, equal]
&
A
    \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha"]
    \arrow[d, "\theta"']
&
B
    \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta"]
    \arrow[d, "f"]
\\
X \arrow[r, "k'"]
&
A' \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha'"]
&
B' \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta'"],
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation*}
if $\varphi\colon B\to T$ and $\varphi'\colon B'\to T$ are the
corresponding acting morphisms, where $T$ is a weak representing
object of $X$, then $\varphi=\varphi'\circ f$. This follows immediately
from the commutativity of the diagram
\[
	\vcenter{\xymatrix{
			\operatorname{SplExt}(B',X)  \ar[d]_-{f^*} \ar[r]^-{\tau_{B'}} & \operatorname{Hom}_\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}(B',T)  \ar[d]^-{-\circ f}\\
			\operatorname{SplExt}(B,X)  \ar[r]_-{\tau_B} & \operatorname{Hom}_\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}(B,T).
	}}
	\]
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\cite{WRA}\label{WRAimpliesAA}
Every weakly action representable category $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ is action accessible.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\cite{WRA}\label{lemmaWRAimpliesAA}
Let $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ be a weakly action representable category, and let
\begin{equation}\label{spltextlemma}
\begin{tikzcd}
X \arrow[r, "k"]
&
A \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha"]
&
B \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta"]
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
be a split extension in $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ with associated acting morphism
$\varphi\colon B\to T$, where $T$ is a weak representing object of
$X$. If $\varphi$ is a monomorphism, then the split
extension~\eqref{spltextlemma} is faithful.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Let
\begin{equation}\label{splextlemma}
\begin{tikzcd}
X \arrow[r, "k'"]
&
A' \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha'"]
&
B' \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta'"]
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
be another split extension in $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$, and suppose that there exist two
morphisms of split extensions
\begin{equation}\label{morphismsplexts}
\begin{tikzcd}
X
    \arrow[r, "k'"]
    \arrow[d, equal]
&
A'
    \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha'"]
    \arrow[d, shift right=.75ex, "\theta"']
    \arrow[d, shift left=.75ex, "\theta'"]
&
B'
    \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta'"]
    \arrow[d, shift right=.75ex, "f"']
    \arrow[d, shift left=.75ex, "g"]
\\
X \arrow[r, "k"]
&
A \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha"]
&
B \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta"].
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
If $\varphi'\colon B'\to T$ is the acting morphism associated with
\eqref{splextlemma}, then
\[
\varphi'=\varphi\circ f=\varphi\circ g. 
\]
Since $\varphi$ is a
monomorphism, it follows that $f=g$. Since $(k',\beta')$ is jointly
strongly epimorphic, we also obtain $\theta=\theta'$. Therefore, \eqref{spltextlemma} is a faithful object of $\ensuremath{\mathsf{SplExt}(X)}$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\cite{WRA}\label{lemma2WRAimpliesAA}
Let $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ be a weakly action representable category, let $B,B'$ and~$X$ be objects of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$, and let $T$ be a weak representing object of
$X$. If $f\colon B\twoheadrightarrow B'$ is a regular epimorphism,
then a morphism $\varphi'\colon B'\to T$ is an acting morphism whenever
$\varphi'\circ f$ is an acting morphism.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Let $\varphi\coloneqq\varphi'\circ f$ be an acting morphism, and let
\begin{equation}\label{splext2lemma2}
\begin{tikzcd}
X \arrow[r, "k"]
&
A \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha"]
&
B \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta"]
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
be a split extension of $B$ by $X$ whose associated acting morphism is~$\varphi$. By~\cite[Proposition~4.4]{WRA}, since $f$ is a regular epimorphism, the diagram
\[
   \vcenter{\xymatrix{
   		\operatorname{SplExt}(B',X)  \ar[d]_-{f^*} \ar[r]^-{\tau_{B'}} & \operatorname{Hom}_\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}(B',T)  \ar[d]^-{-\circ f}\\
   		\operatorname{SplExt}(B,X)  \ar[r]_-{\tau_B} & \operatorname{Hom}_\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}(B,T)
   }}
   \]
is a pullback. Therefore, there exists a split extension
\begin{equation}\label{splextlemma2}
\begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r]
&
X \arrow[r, "k'"]
&
A' \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha'"]
&
B' \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta'"] \arrow[r]
&
0
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
such that $f^*$ sends the isomorphism class of
\eqref{splextlemma2} to that of \eqref{splext2lemma2}, and
$\varphi'$ is the acting morphism associated with
\eqref{splextlemma2}.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Let $X$ be an object of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$, and let
\[
\tau\colon
\operatorname{SplExt}(-,X)
\rightarrowtail
\operatorname{Hom}_{\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}}(-,T)
\]
be a weak representation of $\operatorname{SplExt}(-,X)$. Let
\begin{equation*}
\begin{tikzcd}
X \arrow[r, "k"]
&
A \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha"]
&
B \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta"]
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation*}
be a split extension in $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ with associated acting morphism
$\varphi\colon B\to T$. We construct a morphism of split extensions
\begin{equation}\label{morphismsplext}
\begin{tikzcd}
X
    \arrow[r, "k"]
    \arrow[d, equal]
&
A
    \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha"]
    \arrow[d, "\theta"']
&
B
    \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta"]
    \arrow[d, "f"]
\\
X \arrow[r, "k'"]
&
A' \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha'"]
&
B' \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta'"]
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
with faithful codomain. Let $B'=\varphi(B)$, and let
$f\colon B\twoheadrightarrow B'$ be the corestriction of $\varphi$ to
its image. Thus, $\varphi=\varphi'\circ f$, where
$\varphi'\colon B'\rightarrowtail T$ is given by the
\emph{(regular epi, mono)-factorisation} of $\varphi$.

By \Cref{lemma2WRAimpliesAA}, $\varphi'$ is an acting morphism and
therefore determines a split extension
\begin{equation}\label{splext2}
\begin{tikzcd}
X \arrow[r, "k'"]
&
A' \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha'"]
&
B' \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta'"].
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
Since $\varphi=\varphi'\circ f$, naturality and injectivity of $\tau$
show that the original split extension is isomorphic to the pullback
of~\eqref{splext2} along $f$. The pullback projection therefore gives
the required morphism $\theta$ in~\eqref{morphismsplext}.

Finally, \eqref{splext2} is faithful because its acting morphism
$\varphi'$ is a monomorphism, by \Cref{lemmaWRAimpliesAA}. Hence,
$\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ is action accessible.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\cite{Gray}\label{Gray}
Let $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ be an action representable category, and let $\mathcal{X}$
be a Birkhoff subcategory of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$. Suppose that there exist
monomorphisms
$m\colon S\rightarrowtail B$ and
$m'\colon S\rightarrowtail B'$ in $\mathcal{X}$, monomorphisms
$u\colon B\rightarrowtail D$ and
$u'\colon B'\rightarrowtail D$ in $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$, an object~$X$ of
$\mathcal{X}$, and a monomorphism $v\colon D\rightarrowtail[X]$ such
that:
\begin{itemize}
\item[(i)] $m$ and $m'$ cannot be amalgamated in $\mathcal{X}$;
\item[(ii)] $u\circ m=u'\circ m'$;
\item[(iii)] the split extensions with kernel $X$ corresponding to
$v \circ u$ and $v \circ u'$ belong to $\mathcal{X}$.
\end{itemize}
Then $\mathcal{X}$ is not weakly action representable.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
\label{remobjects}
Action representability and weak action representability are properties of the whole category~$\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$: they require a representation, or a weak representation, of $\operatorname{SplExt}(-,X)$ for every object~$X$ of~$\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$. For a single object~$X$, one may still ask whether $\operatorname{SplExt}(-,X)$ is representable, that is, whether $X$ admits an actor. A category which is not action representable may contain many such objects. For instance, an object $X$ of $\ensuremath{\mathsf{CAssoc}}$ admits an actor if and only if $\operatorname{M}(X)$ is commutative~\cite[Theorem~2.6]{BJK2}, and every associative algebra $X$ with $\operatorname{Ann}(X)=0$, or with $X^2=X$, has actor $\operatorname{Bim}(X)$; see the discussion following \Cref{actmorpAssoc}. More generally, the objects of an Orzech category of interest which admit an actor are characterised in terms of their universal strict general actor; see \Cref{SecUSGA}. On the other hand, to show that a category is not weakly action representable, it suffices to exhibit a single object~$X$ for which $\operatorname{SplExt}(-,X)$ admits no weak representation. This is the strategy underlying \Cref{Gray}.
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- head-1: body_md -->
for every $*\in\Omega_2'$,
$x_1+(x_2*x_3)=(x_2*x_3)+x_1$.
<!-- end head-1 -->
<!-- head-2: body_md -->
for every ordered pair
$(*,\bar{*})\in\Omega_2'\times\Omega_2'$, there exists a word $W$ in the expressions
\[
x_1(x_2x_3),\,
x_1(x_3x_2),\,
(x_2x_3)x_1,\,
(x_3x_2)x_1,
\]
\[
x_2(x_1x_3),\,
x_2(x_3x_1),\,
(x_1x_3)x_2,\,
(x_3x_1)x_2,
\]
where each juxtaposition represents an operation in $\Omega_2'$. Denoting
this word by $W(x_1;x_2,x_3;*,\bar{*})$, the identity
\[
(x_1*x_2)\mathbin{\bar{*}}x_3
=
W(x_1;x_2,x_3;*,\bar{*})
\]
belongs to $\mathbb{E}$.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end head-2 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
\cite{Orzech}\label{definterest}
A category of groups with operations satisfying conditions~(i)--(vi)
is called an \emph{Orzech category of interest}, or simply a
\emph{category of interest}.
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- remark-8: body_md -->
If $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ is a category of abelian groups with operations, that is, if
the group operation $+$ is commutative, then Axiom~1 is automatically
satisfied.
<!-- end remark-8 -->
<!-- example-6: body_md -->
The categories $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Grp}}$ of groups and $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Ab}}$ of abelian groups are Orzech
categories of interest, with
$\Omega_1'=\Omega_2'=\emptyset$.
<!-- end example-6 -->
<!-- example-7: body_md -->
The categories $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Rng}}$ of not necessarily unital rings and $\ensuremath{\mathsf{CRng}}$ of
commutative not necessarily unital rings are Orzech categories of
interest, with $\Omega_1'=\emptyset$ and
$\Omega_2'=\{*,*^{\operatorname{op}}\}$, where $*$ denotes ring multiplication.
Axiom~2 is given by the associativity of $*$.
<!-- end example-7 -->
<!-- example-8: body_md -->
Let $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}=\ensuremath{\mathsf{Alg}}$ be the category of \emph{all} non-associative algebras
over a field $\mathbb{F}$. Its objects are $\mathbb{F}$-vector spaces $X$ endowed
with a bilinear operation
\[
X\times X\to X\colon(x,y)\mapsto x*y,
\]
and its morphisms are the $\mathbb{F}$-linear maps preserving this operation.
The category $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Alg}}$ is a category of abelian groups with operations,
with $\Omega_1'=\{\omega_\lambda\mid\lambda\in\mathbb{F}\}$, where
$\omega_\lambda(x)=\lambda x$, and $\Omega_2'=\{*,*^{\operatorname{op}}\}$, but it is not an Orzech category of
interest because Axiom~2 fails.
<!-- end example-8 -->
<!-- example-9: body_md -->
\label{exalgint}
The category $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Assoc}}$ of associative algebras over a field $\mathbb{F}$ is an
Orzech category of interest. Its underlying group operation is
commutative, and
\[
\Omega_1'=\{\omega_\lambda\mid\lambda\in\mathbb{F}\},
\qquad
\Omega_2'=\left\{\ast,\ast^{\operatorname{op}}\right\},
\]
where $\ast$ denotes the associative multiplication. Axiom~2 is given
by associativity:
\[
(x_1\ast x_2)\ast x_3=x_1\ast(x_2\ast x_3).
\]
Similarly, the category $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Lie}}$ of Lie algebras over $\mathbb{F}$ is an Orzech
category of interest, with
\[
\Omega_1'=\{\omega_\lambda\mid\lambda\in\mathbb{F}\},
\qquad
\Omega_2'=
\left\{[-,-],[-,-]^{\operatorname{op}}\right\},
\]
where $[-,-]$ denotes the Lie bracket, and Axiom~2 is given by the
Jacobi identity. Moreover, $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Assoc}}'=\ensuremath{\mathsf{Lie}}'=\ensuremath{\mathsf{Alg}}$.
<!-- end example-9 -->
<!-- example-10: body_md -->
\label{esLeib}
A \emph{(right) Leibniz algebra}~\cite{loday} over a field $\mathbb{F}$ is a
non-associative $\mathbb{F}$-algebra $X$ endowed with a bilinear operation
$[-,-]$ satisfying the \emph{(right) Leibniz identity}
\[
[[x,y],z]=[[x,z],y]+[x,[y,z]]
\]
for all $x,y,z\in X$. Lie algebras are precisely the Leibniz algebras with an alternating bracket, that is, $[x,x]=0$ for every $x \in X$, since in this case the Leibniz identity is equivalent to the Jacobi identity.

The category $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Leib}}$ of right Leibniz algebras over $\mathbb{F}$ is an Orzech
category of interest, with
\[
\Omega_1'=\{\omega_\lambda\mid\lambda\in\mathbb{F}\},
\qquad
\Omega_2'=
\left\{[-,-],[-,-]^{\operatorname{op}}\right\}.
\]
Axiom~2 may be expressed through the rewriting identities
\begin{align*}
[x,[y,z]]
  &= [[x,y],z]-[[x,z],y],\\
[[y,z],x]
  &= [[y,x],z]-[y,[x,z]],
\end{align*}
the first of which is equivalent to the Leibniz identity, while the second one follows from it, since $[x,[y,z]+[z,y]]=0$ in every right Leibniz algebra. Moreover, $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Leib}}'=\ensuremath{\mathsf{Alg}}$.
<!-- end example-10 -->
<!-- example-11: body_md -->
Recall that a \emph{Jordan algebra} over a field $\mathbb{F}$ is a
commutative, not necessarily associative, $\mathbb{F}$-algebra $(X,\cdot)$
satisfying the \emph{Jordan identity}
\[
(xy)(xx)=x(y(xx))
\]
for all $x,y\in X$.

The category $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Jord}}$ of Jordan algebras over $\mathbb{F}$ is not an Orzech
category of interest because Axiom~2 is not satisfied.
<!-- end example-11 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
\label{def derived actions}
Let $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ be a category of groups with operations, and let
\begin{equation}\label{diag:SplC}
\begin{tikzcd}
X \arrow[r, "k"]
&
A \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha"]
&
B \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta"]
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
be a split extension in $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$. The collection of maps
\[
\{f_*\colon B\times X\to X\mid *\in\Omega_2\}
\]
defined by
\begin{equation*}
b\cdot x
=
k^{-1}\bigl(\beta(b)+k(x)-\beta(b)\bigr),
\qquad
b*x
=
k^{-1}\bigl(\beta(b)*k(x)\bigr),
\end{equation*}
where
\[
b\cdot x\coloneqq f_+(b,x),
\quad
b*x\coloneqq f_*(b,x)
\quad
\text{for every }*\in\Omega_2',
\]
is called the \emph{derived action} of $B$ on $X$ associated
with~\eqref{diag:SplC}. These expressions are well defined, since $\beta(b)+k(x)-\beta(b)$ and $\beta(b)*k(x)$ belong to
$\ker(\alpha)$, and the corestriction
$k\colon X\to\ker(\alpha)$ is an isomorphism.
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
The algebra on $B\times X$ defined by these operations is called the \emph{semidirect product} of $B$ and $X$ with respect to $\{f_*\}_{*\in\Omega_2}$, and it is denoted by $B\ltimes X$.
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- remark-9: body_md -->
\label{remabgrps}
When the group operation $+$ is commutative, one has $b\cdot x=x$ for
all~$b\in B$ and $x\in X$. Hence, a derived action of $B$ on $X$ is
determined by a collection of maps
$\{f_*\}_{*\in\Omega_2'}$.
<!-- end remark-9 -->
<!-- example-12: body_md -->
Let $B$ and $X$ be objects of $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Alg}}$. A pair of maps
\[
f_*\colon B\times X\to X,
\qquad
f_{*^{\operatorname{op}}}\colon B\times X\to X
\]
defines a derived action of $B$ on $X$ if and only if the vector space
$B\oplus X\cong B\times X$, endowed with the binary operation
\[
(b,x)*(b',x')
=
(b*b',x*x'+b*x'+x*b'),
\]
is a non-associative algebra over $\mathbb{F}$. This happens if and only if
$f_*$ and $f_{*^{\operatorname{op}}}$ are bilinear maps.
<!-- end example-12 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\cite{Casas, Datuashvili}\label{conditionactions}
Let $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ be an Orzech category of interest, and let $B$ and $X$ be
objects of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$. Consider a collection of maps
$\{f_*\colon B\times X\to X\mid *\in\Omega_2\}$, denoted by
\[
b\cdot x\coloneqq f_+(b,x),
\qquad
b*x\coloneqq f_*(b,x)
\quad
\text{for every }*\in\Omega_2'.
\]
This collection defines a derived action of $B$ on $X$ in $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ if
and only if the following conditions are satisfied:
\begin{itemize}
\item[(1)] $0\cdot x=x$;
\item[(2)] $b\cdot(x+x')=b\cdot x+b\cdot x'$;
\item[(3)] $(b+b')\cdot x=b\cdot(b'\cdot x)$;
\item[(4)] $b*(x+x')=b*x+b*x'$;
\item[(5)] $(b+b')*x=b*x+b'*x$;
\item[(6)] $b\cdot(x*x')=x*x'$;
\item[(7)] $b\cdot(x*b')=x*b'$;
\item[(8)] $(b*b')\cdot x=x$;
\item[(9)] $x*(b\cdot x')=x*x'$;
\item[(10)] $b*(b'\cdot x)=b*x$;
\item[(11)] $\omega(b\cdot x)=\omega(b)\cdot\omega(x)$;
\item[(12)] $\omega(x*b)=\omega(x)*b=x*\omega(b)$;
\item[(13)] $\alpha+\beta*\gamma=\beta*\gamma+\alpha$;
\item[(14)] for every ordered pair
$(*,\bar{*})\in\Omega_2'\times\Omega_2'$, the word $W$ fixed in Axiom~2 satisfies
\[
(x_1*x_2)\mathbin{\bar{*}}x_3
=
W(x_1;x_2,x_3;*,\bar{*});
\]
\item[(15)] every remaining identity in the fixed defining
presentation of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ holds in $B\ltimes X$, evaluated on
arbitrary elements of $B\times X$.
\end{itemize}
These conditions hold for all $\omega\in\Omega_1'$,
$*\in\Omega_2'$, $b,b'\in B$, $x,x'\in X$, and
$\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in B\cup X$ for which the expressions involved are defined, as well as for all $x_1,x_2,x_3\in B\cup X$ for which both sides of~(14) are defined.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{conditionsactionsabgroups}
Let $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ be an Orzech category of interest whose objects are abelian
groups with operations, and let $B$ and $X$ be objects of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$. A
collection of maps
$\{f_*\colon B\times X\to X\mid *\in\Omega_2'\}$, together with the
trivial group action $b\cdot x=x$, defines a derived
action of $B$ on $X$ in $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ if and only if it satisfies
conditions~(4), (5), (12), (14), and~(15) of
\Cref{conditionactions}.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- example-13: body_md -->
Let $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}=\ensuremath{\mathsf{Lie}}$. Since the group operation $+$ is commutative, a
derived action of $B$ on $X$ is given by a pair of bilinear maps
\[
f_{[-,-]}\colon B\times X\to X,
\qquad
f_{[-,-]^{\operatorname{op}}}\colon B\times X\to X
\]
such that the bilinear operation
\[
[(b,x),(b',x')]
=
([b,b'],[x,x']+[b,x']+[x,b'])
\]
defines a Lie algebra structure on the vector space
$B\oplus X$, where
$[b,x]\coloneqq f_{[-,-]}(b,x)$ and
$[x,b]\coloneqq f_{[-,-]^{\operatorname{op}}}(b,x)$. By
\Cref{conditionsactionsabgroups}, this happens if and only if
\begin{itemize}
\item[(1)] $[x,b]=-[b,x]$;
\item[(2)] $[b,[x,x']]=[[b,x],x']+[x,[b,x']]$;
\item[(3)] $[[b,b'],x]=[b,[b',x]]-[b',[b,x]]$;
\end{itemize}
for all $b,b'\in B$ and $x,x'\in X$.

For instance, let $B=\operatorname{Der}(X)$ be the Lie algebra of derivations of
$X$. The canonical action of $\operatorname{Der}(X)$ on $X$, defined by
\[
[d,x]=-[x,d]\coloneqq d(x),
\]
for all $d\in\operatorname{Der}(X)$ and $x\in X$, is a derived action in $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Lie}}$,
since the semidirect product
$\operatorname{Hol}(X)=\operatorname{Der}(X)\ltimes X$, the holomorph of $X$, is a
Lie algebra.

By contrast, the canonical action of $\operatorname{Bim}(X)$ on $X$ in $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Assoc}}$,
defined by
\[
(f\ast-,-\ast f)\ast x\coloneqq f\ast x
\]
and
\[
x\ast(f\ast-,-\ast f)\coloneqq x\ast f,
\]
for all $(f\ast-,-\ast f)\in\operatorname{Bim}(X)$ and $x\in X$, is not, in
general, a derived action. Indeed, a derived action of $B$ on $X$ in
$\ensuremath{\mathsf{Assoc}}$ is given by a pair of bilinear maps
\[
f_*\colon B\times X\to X,
\qquad
f_{*^{\operatorname{op}}}\colon B\times X\to X,
\]
with $b*x\coloneqq f_*(b,x)$ and
$x*b\coloneqq f_{*^{\operatorname{op}}}(b,x)$, satisfying
\begin{itemize}
\item[(1)] $(b*b')*x=b*(b'*x)$;
\item[(2)] $x*(b*b')=(x*b)*b'$;
\item[(3)] $(b*x)*b'=b*(x*b')$;
\item[(4)] $b*(x*x')=(b*x)*x'$;
\item[(5)] $(x*x')*b=x*(x'*b)$;
\item[(6)] $x*(b*x')=(x*b)*x'$;
\end{itemize}
for all $b,b'\in B$ and $x,x'\in X$.

As observed in \Cref{exnotacting}, given
$(f\ast-,-\ast f),(f'\ast-,-\ast f')\in\operatorname{Bim}(X)$, condition~(3) need
not be satisfied. For instance, let $X=\mathbb{F}\{e_1,e_2\}$ be the
two-dimensional abelian algebra, and let
$(f\ast-,-\ast f)\in\operatorname{Bim}(X)
=\operatorname{End}(X)\times\operatorname{End}(X)^{\operatorname{op}}$ be defined by
\[
f\ast e_1=e_2,
\quad
f\ast e_2=0,
\qquad
e_1\ast f=e_1,
\quad
e_2\ast f=e_1.
\]
Then
\[
(f\ast e_1)\ast f
=
e_2\ast f
=
e_1
\neq
e_2
=
f\ast e_1
=
f\ast(e_1\ast f).
\]
Thus, condition~(3) is not satisfied, and the semidirect product
$\operatorname{Bim}(X)\ltimes X$ is not an associative algebra. This gives an
example of a derived action in $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Assoc}}'=\ensuremath{\mathsf{Alg}}$ that is not a derived
action in $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Assoc}}$.
<!-- end example-13 -->
<!-- remark-10: body_md -->
\label{reminterest}
In summary, derived actions can be defined in any category of groups
with operations and can be used to describe split extensions, or
equivalently internal actions.

Starting with a split extension in $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$
\begin{equation}\label{splrem}
\begin{tikzcd}
X \arrow[r, "k"]
&
A \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha"]
&
B \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta"],
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
we obtain the derived action
$\{f_*\}_{*\in\Omega_2}$ of $B$ on $X$ given by
\begin{equation*}
b\cdot x
=
k^{-1}\bigl(\beta(b)+k(x)-\beta(b)\bigr),
\qquad
b*x
=
k^{-1}\bigl(\beta(b)*k(x)\bigr),
\end{equation*}
for all $b\in B$, $x\in X$, and $*\in\Omega_2'$. Conversely, given a
collection of maps $\{f_*\}_{*\in\Omega_2}$, denoted by
\[
b\cdot x\coloneqq f_+(b,x),
\qquad
b*x\coloneqq f_*(b,x)
\quad
\text{for every }*\in\Omega_2',
\]
one can construct the semidirect product $B\ltimes X$ as above. The
collection $\{f_*\}_{*\in\Omega_2}$ is a derived action of $B$ on $X$
if and only if $B\ltimes X$ is an object of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$. In this case, it is
the derived action associated with the split extension
\begin{equation}\label{splrem2}
\begin{tikzcd}
X \arrow[r, "\iota_2"]
&
B\ltimes X \arrow[r, shift left, "\pi_1"]
&
B \arrow[l, shift left, "\iota_1"],
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
where $\iota_2(x)=(0,x)$, $\iota_1(b)=(b,0)$, and $\pi_1(b,x)=b$. Moreover, the diagram
\begin{equation*}
\begin{tikzcd}
X
    \arrow[r, "\iota_2"]
    \arrow[d, equal]
&
B\ltimes X
    \arrow[r, shift left, "\pi_1"]
    \arrow[d, "\theta"']
&
B
    \arrow[l, shift left, "\iota_1"]
    \arrow[d, equal]
\\
X \arrow[r, "k"]
&
A \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha"]
&
B \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta"],
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation*}
where $\theta(b,x)=k(x)+\beta(b)$, defines an isomorphism of split extensions between~\eqref{splrem2} and~\eqref{splrem}.

We therefore obtain a bijection
\[
\eta_B\colon\operatorname{SplExt}(B,X)\cong\operatorname{DAct}(B,X)
\]
between the set $\operatorname{SplExt}(B,X)$ of isomorphism classes of split
extensions of $B$ by $X$ in $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ and the set $\operatorname{DAct}(B,X)$ of derived
actions of $B$ on $X$ in $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$.
<!-- end remark-10 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{intderivedact}
The bijections $\eta_B$ extend to a natural isomorphism of functors
\[
\eta\colon\operatorname{SplExt}(-,X)\cong\operatorname{DAct}(-,X).
\]
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Let $g\colon B'\to B$ be a morphism in $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$, and suppose that:
\begin{itemize}
\item[(1)] $g^*=\operatorname{SplExt}(g,X)$ sends the isomorphism class of
\eqref{splBint} to that of \eqref{splB'int};
\item[(2)] $\operatorname{DAct}(g,X)$ sends the derived action
$\{f_*\}_{*\in\Omega_2}$ associated with \eqref{splBint} to the
collection $\{f'_*\}_{*\in\Omega_2}$ defined
in~\eqref{eqf'}.
\end{itemize}
Let $\theta\colon A'\to A$ be the morphism obtained by pulling back
along $g$. Then
\[
\theta\circ\beta'=\beta\circ g \quad \text{ and } \quad \theta\circ k'=k.
\]
We must show that $\eta_B$ is natural in $B$, that is, that the
following diagram in $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Set}}$ commutes:
\[
\vcenter{\xymatrix{
		\operatorname{SplExt}(B,X)  \ar[d]_-{g^*} \ar[r]^-{\eta_{B}} & \operatorname{DAct}(B,X)  \ar[d]^-{\operatorname{DAct}(g,X)}\\
		\operatorname{SplExt}(B',X)  \ar[r]_-{\eta_{B'}} & \operatorname{DAct}(B',X).
}}
\]
The composition $\eta_{B'}\circ g^*$ sends~\eqref{splBint} to the derived action of $B'$ on $X$ associated with
\eqref{splB'int}. Denote this derived action by
$\{F'_*\}_{*\in\Omega_2}$, so that
\[
k'\bigl(F'_+(b',x)\bigr)
=
\beta'(b')+k'(x)-\beta'(b'),
\qquad
k'\bigl(F'_*(b',x)\bigr)
=
\beta'(b')*k'(x),
\]
for all $b'\in B'$, $x\in X$, and $*\in\Omega_2'$. Applying $\theta$
and using the pullback identities, we obtain
\begin{align*}
k\bigl(F'_+(b',x)\bigr)
&=\theta\bigl(k'(F'_+(b',x))\bigr)\\
&=\theta\bigl(\beta'(b')+k'(x)-\beta'(b')\bigr)\\
&=\beta(g(b'))+k(x)-\beta(g(b'))\\
&=k\bigl(f_+(g(b'),x)\bigr)\\
&=k\bigl(f'_+(b',x)\bigr)
\end{align*}
and
\begin{align*}
k\bigl(F'_*(b',x)\bigr)
&=\theta\bigl(k'(F'_*(b',x))\bigr)\\
&=\theta\bigl(\beta'(b')*k'(x)\bigr)\\
&=\beta(g(b'))*k(x)\\
&=k\bigl(f_*(g(b'),x)\bigr)\\
&=k\bigl(f'_*(b',x)\bigr).
\end{align*}
Since $k$ is injective, it follows that
\[
F'_+(b',x)=f_+(g(b'),x)=f'_+(b',x),
\qquad
F'_*(b',x)=f_*(g(b'),x)=f'_*(b',x)
\]
for every $*\in\Omega_2'$. Therefore,
$\eta_{B'}\circ g^*=\operatorname{DAct}(g,X)\circ\eta_B$, proving that
\[
\eta\colon\operatorname{SplExt}(-,X)\cong\operatorname{DAct}(-,X)
\]
is a natural isomorphism.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\cite{Casas}
Let $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ be an Orzech category of interest, and let $X$ be an object
of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$. Then $X$ admits an actor $[X]$ if and only if the semidirect
product $\operatorname{USGA}(X)\ltimes X$ is an object of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$. In this case,
$[X]\cong\operatorname{USGA}(X)$.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\cite{CigoliManciniMetere}\label{USGA}
Let $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ be an Orzech category of interest, and let $X$ be an object
of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$. There exists a natural monomorphism of functors
\[
\mu\colon
\operatorname{SplExt}(-,X)
\rightarrowtail
\operatorname{Hom}_{\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}'}\bigl(U(-),\operatorname{USGA}(X)\bigr),
\]
where $\operatorname{SplExt}(-,X)=\operatorname{SplExt}_{\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}}(-,X)$ and
$U\colon\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}\to\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}'$ is the forgetful functor. If, moreover,
$\operatorname{USGA}(X)$ is an object of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$, then $(\operatorname{USGA}(X),\mu)$ is a weak
representation of $\operatorname{SplExt}(-,X)$.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\cite{CigoliManciniMetere}\label{USGA2}
Let $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ be an Orzech category of interest. If $\operatorname{USGA}(X)$ is an
object of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ for every object $X$ of $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$, then $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ is weakly action
representable.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- example-14: body_md -->
\label{USGA_Assoc}
Let $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}=\ensuremath{\mathsf{Assoc}}$, with the presentation of Axiom~2 given in \Cref{exalgint}, and let $X$ be an associative algebra. By~\cite[Proposition~5.6]{Casas}, the universal strict general actor $\operatorname{USGA}(X)$ is an associative algebra. Hence, by \Cref{USGA2}, the category $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Assoc}}$ is 
weakly action representable~\cite{WRA}.
<!-- end example-14 -->
<!-- example-15: body_md -->
\label{USGA_Leib}
Let $\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}=\ensuremath{\mathsf{Leib}}$, and let $X$ be a Leibniz algebra. With the presentation of Axiom~2 adopted above, the universal strict general actor $\operatorname{USGA}(X)$ is a Leibniz algebra; see~\cite[Proposition~5.4]{Casas}. Hence, by \Cref{USGA2}, the category $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Leib}}$ is weakly action representable. We refer the reader to~\cite{CigoliManciniMetere} for an explicit description of a weak
representation and of the corresponding acting morphisms in $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Leib}}$.
<!-- end example-15 -->
<!-- remark-11: body_md -->
\label{presUSGA}
Condition~(3) in the construction of $\operatorname{USGA}$ is expressed in terms of
the identities appearing in Axiom~2. An Orzech category of interest
$\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}$ may admit several equivalent presentations, obtained by
choosing different but equivalent systems of identities for Axiom~2.
The construction of the universal strict general actor may therefore
depend on the chosen presentation: equivalent presentations of the
same category may give rise to non-isomorphic universal strict general
actors.
<!-- end remark-11 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
\cite{VdL-NAA}
A \emph{non-associative algebra} $X$ is a vector space over a field
$\mathbb{F}$ endowed with a bilinear operation
$X\times X\to X\colon(x,y)\mapsto xy$, called the
\emph{multiplication} or \emph{product}.
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- remark-12: body_md -->
In some cases, such as Lie and Leibniz algebras, we refer to the
multiplication as the \emph{commutator} or \emph{bracket} and denote
it by $[x,y]$.
<!-- end remark-12 -->
<!-- remark-13: body_md -->
We do not assume the existence of a unit or impose any other condition
on the multiplication besides bilinearity.
<!-- end remark-13 -->
<!-- definition-9: body_md -->
Let $S$ be a set. An element $\varphi\in\mathbb{F}[\operatorname{FM}(S)]$ is called a
\emph{non-associative polynomial} in the variables $S$. A non-zero
polynomial is called a \emph{monomial} if it is a scalar multiple of
an element of $\operatorname{FM}(S)$.
<!-- end definition-9 -->
<!-- remark-14: body_md -->
Since our algebras need not have units, the polynomials considered
here have no constant terms.
<!-- end remark-14 -->
<!-- definition-10: body_md -->
\label{def identity variety}
A \emph{(polynomial) identity} of an algebra $X$ is a non-associative
polynomial $\varphi=\varphi(x_1,\dots,x_n)$ such that
\[
\varphi(a_1,\dots,a_n)=0
\]
for all $a_1,\dots,a_n\in X$. In this case, we say that $X$
satisfies the identity $\varphi$.
<!-- end definition-10 -->
<!-- definition-11: body_md -->
Let $S$ be a set of variables and let
$I\subseteq\mathbb{F}[\operatorname{FM}(S)]$. The \emph{variety of non-associative
algebras determined by $I$} is the class of all non-associative
$\mathbb{F}$-algebras satisfying every identity in $I$. More generally, we
say that a variety satisfies the identities in $I$ if every algebra
in the variety satisfies them.
<!-- end definition-11 -->
<!-- definition-12: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{V}$ be a variety of non-associative algebras. We say that:
\begin{itemize}
\item[(1)] $\mathcal{V}$ is \emph{homogeneous} if it is determined by a set of
homogeneous identities;
\item[(2)] $\mathcal{V}$ is \emph{operadic}~\cite{Cosmash} if it is determined by a set of multilinear identities.
\item[(3)] $\mathcal{V}$ is \emph{quadratic} if there exists a set of identities of degrees~$2$ and~$3$ generating all the identities of $\mathcal{V}$\footnote{Recall that an operadic, quadratic variety of algebras over a field can be viewed as a variety determined by a quadratic \emph{operad}; see~\cite{DGM}.}.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end definition-12 -->
<!-- remark-15: body_md -->
If the field $\mathbb{F}$ is infinite, then every variety $\mathcal{V}$ of non-associative $\mathbb{F}$-algebras is homogeneous~\cite{hmg}. Moreover,
if $\operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F})=0$, then every such variety is operadic. This follows
from the multilinearisation process; see
\cite[Corollary~3.7]{Osborn}.
<!-- end remark-15 -->
<!-- remark-16: body_md -->
When considering a variety $\mathcal{V}$ of non-associative algebras, we assume
that the field $\mathbb{F}$ is fixed and omit it from the notation.
<!-- end remark-16 -->
<!-- examples-1: body_md -->
\label{Examples varieties}{\ }
\begin{enumerate}
\item The variety $\ensuremath{\mathsf{AbAlg}}$ of \emph{abelian algebras} is determined
by the identity $xy=0$. Recall that $\ensuremath{\mathsf{AbAlg}}$ is an abelian category
and is therefore action representable.

\item $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Com}}$ and $\ensuremath{\mathsf{ACom}}$ denote, respectively, the
varieties of commutative and anti-commutative, not necessarily
associative, algebras. These varieties are determined, respectively,
by the identities $xy-yx=0$ and $xy+yx=0$.

\item The variety $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Assoc}}$ of associative algebras is determined by
the identity $x(yz)-(xy)z=0$.

\item The variety $\ensuremath{\mathsf{CAssoc}}$ of commutative associative algebras is
determined by the identities $xy-yx=0$ and $x(yz)-(xy)z=0$.

\item The variety $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Alt}}$ of \emph{alternative algebras} is determined
by the identities
\[
(yx)x-y(xx)=0
\quad\text{and}\quad
x(xy)-(xx)y=0.
\]
Every associative algebra is alternative, while the algebra of \emph{octonions} provides an example of an alternative algebra that is not associative.

\item The variety $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Lie}}$ of \emph{Lie algebras} is characterised by
$xx=0$ together with the \emph{Jacobi identity}
$x(yz)+y(zx)+z(xy)=0$. If $\operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F})\neq2$, the condition $xx=0$
is equivalent to anti-commutativity. Hence, in this case, the variety
$\ensuremath{\mathsf{Lie}}$ is operadic.

\item The variety $\ensuremath{\mathsf{JJord}}$ of \emph{Jacobi--Jordan
algebras}~\cite{Burde} is determined by commutativity and the Jacobi
identity. Jacobi--Jordan algebras, also known as \emph{mock-Lie
algebras}, are the commutative counterpart of Lie algebras.

\item The variety $\ensuremath{\mathsf{ACAAssoc}}$ of anti-commutative anti-associative
algebras is determined by the identities
$xy+yx=0$ and $x(yz)+(xy)z=0$.

\item The variety $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Leib}}$ of right Leibniz algebras is determined by
the right Leibniz identity
$(xy)z-(xz)y-x(yz)=0$.

\item The category $\ensuremath{\mathsf{BoolRng}}$ of not necessarily unital
\emph{Boolean rings} is the variety of commutative
associative $\mathbb{F}_2$-algebras satisfying $xx=x$. Notice that this
variety is not homogeneous.

\item Given a variety $\mathcal{V}$, one can define its subvariety
$\ensuremath{\mathsf{Nil}}_k(\mathcal{V})$ of $k$-\emph{nilpotent} algebras by adjoining to the
identities of $\mathcal{V}$ the identities
\[
x_1x_2\cdots x_{k+1}=0,
\]
for all possible choices of parentheses. For instance, if
$\mathcal{V}=\ensuremath{\mathsf{CAssoc}}$, every non-trivial multilinear identity of degree
$k+1$ is equivalent to $x_1\cdots x_{k+1}=0$. Hence, the only
proper operadic subvarieties of $\ensuremath{\mathsf{CAssoc}}$ are the varieties of
$k$-nilpotent commutative associative algebras.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end examples-1 -->
<!-- definition-13: body_md -->
\label{Rem:SplitV}
Let $\mathcal{V}$ be a variety of non-associative algebras, and let
\begin{equation}\label{diag:SplV}
\begin{tikzcd}
X \arrow[r, "k"]
&
A \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha"]
&
B \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta"]
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
be a split extension in $\mathcal{V}$. The pair of bilinear maps
\[
l\colon B\times X\to X,
\qquad
r\colon X\times B\to X
\]
defined by
\[
b\ast x
=
k^{-1}\bigl(\beta(b)k(x)\bigr)
\quad\text{and}\quad
x\ast b
=
k^{-1}\bigl(k(x)\beta(b)\bigr),
\]
for all $b\in B$ and $x\in X$, where
$b\ast-=l(b,-)$ and $-\ast b=r(-,b)$, is called the
\emph{derived action} of $B$ on $X$ associated
with~\eqref{diag:SplV}.
<!-- end definition-13 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lemma derived action}
Let $\mathcal{V}$ be a variety of non-associative algebras. A pair of bilinear
maps $(l,r)$ defines a derived action of $B$ on $X$ if and only if
$(B\oplus X,\cdot)$ is an object of $\mathcal{V}$. In this case,
$(B\oplus X,\cdot)$ is called the \emph{semidirect product} of $B$
and $X$ with respect to the derived action $(l,r)$ and is denoted by
$B\ltimes X$.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- remark-17: body_md -->
\label{rem_b*}
For every split extension~\eqref{diag:SplV} and its associated derived
action $(l,r)$, there is an isomorphism of split extensions
\begin{equation*}
\begin{tikzcd}
X
    \arrow[r, "\iota_2"]
    \arrow[d, equal]
&
B\ltimes X
    \arrow[r, shift left, "\pi_1"]
    \arrow[d, "\theta"']
&
B
    \arrow[l, shift left, "\iota_1"]
    \arrow[d, equal]
\\
X \arrow[r, "k"]
&
A \arrow[r, shift left, "\alpha"]
&
B \arrow[l, shift left, "\beta"],
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation*}
where
$\theta\colon B\ltimes X\to A\colon
(b,x)\mapsto\beta(b)+k(x)$.
Thus, when writing $b\ast x$ (respectively, $x\ast b$), one may
identify it with the product $(b,0)\cdot(0,x)$ (respectively,
$(0,x)\cdot(b,0)$) in $B\ltimes X$.
<!-- end remark-17 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\cite{Tim,GMVdL,Coco_Thesis}\label{Theorem AC iff Orzech}
Let $\mathcal{V}$ be a homogeneous variety of non-associative algebras. The following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{tfae}
\item $\mathcal{V}$ is action accessible.
\item $\mathcal{V}$ is an Orzech category of interest.
\item There exist
$\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_8,\mu_1,\dots,\mu_8\in\mathbb{F}$ such that
\begin{align*}
x(yz)
={}&\lambda_1(xy)z+\lambda_2(yx)z+\lambda_3z(xy)+\lambda_4z(yx)\\
&+\lambda_5(xz)y+\lambda_6(zx)y+\lambda_7y(xz)+\lambda_8y(zx)
\intertext{and}
(yz)x
={}&\mu_1(xy)z+\mu_2(yx)z+\mu_3z(xy)+\mu_4z(yx)\\
&+\mu_5(xz)y+\mu_6(zx)y+\mu_7y(xz)+\mu_8y(zx)
\end{align*}
are identities of $\mathcal{V}$. These two identities are together called the
\emph{$\lambda/\mu$-rules}.
\end{tfae}
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- remark-18: body_md -->
\label{USGA_var}
For varieties of non-associative algebras, the existence of the
$\lambda/\mu$-rules is precisely the formulation of Axiom~2 for an
Orzech category of interest. Indeed, in this setting, Axiom~2 requires
the products $x(yz)$ and $(yz)x$ to be expressible as linear
combinations of the eight products appearing on the corresponding
right-hand sides above. Thus, condition~(iii) of \Cref{Theorem AC iff Orzech} is equivalent to Axiom~2. These
expressions need not be unique; throughout the text, we fix a choice
of the $\lambda/\mu$-rules for each variety under consideration.
<!-- end remark-18 -->
<!-- remark-19: body_md -->
If $\mathcal{V}$ is an action accessible variety of commutative or anti-commutative algebras, then the $\lambda/\mu$-rules can be chosen of the form
\[
x(yz)=\alpha(xy)z+\beta(xz)y,
\qquad
(yz)x=\varepsilon\alpha(xy)z+\varepsilon\beta(xz)y,
\]
for some $\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{F}$, where $\varepsilon=1$ in the commutative case and $\varepsilon=-1$ in the anti-commutative case. Indeed, by (anti-)commutativity, each of the eight products on the right-hand sides of the $\lambda/\mu$-rules is equal to $\pm(xy)z$ or to $\pm(xz)y$, and $(yz)x=\varepsilon\,x(yz)$.
<!-- end remark-19 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\cite{Tim}\label{AR implies Lie}
Let $\mathbb{F}$ be an infinite field with $\operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F}) \neq 2$, and let $\mathcal{V}$ be a non-trivial variety of non-associative $\mathbb{F}$-algebras. The following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{tfae}
\item $\mathcal{V}$ is action representable.
\item $\mathcal{V}$ is either the variety $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Lie}}$ or the variety $\ensuremath{\mathsf{AbAlg}}$.
\end{tfae}
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\cite{WRAAlg}
Let $\mathcal{V}$ be a weakly action representable variety of
non-associative algebras, and let $X$ be an object of $\mathcal{V}$.
Fix a weak representation $(T,\tau)$ of $\operatorname{SplExt}(-,X)$. Given an object $B$ of $\mathcal{V}$ and a derived action
\[
l\colon B\times X\to X,
\qquad
r\colon X\times B\to X,
\]
let $\varphi\colon B\to T$ be the corresponding acting morphism.
Then $\varphi(B)$ admits a unique derived action $(l',r')$ on $X$
such that
\[
l'(\varphi(b),x)=l(b,x)
\quad\text{and}\quad
r'(x,\varphi(b))=r(x,b)
\]
for every $b\in B$ and $x\in X$.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Write $\varphi=j\circ q$, where
$q\colon B\twoheadrightarrow\varphi(B)$ is the corestriction of
$\varphi$ to its image and
$j\colon\varphi(B)\rightarrowtail T$ is the inclusion.
Since $j\circ q$ is an acting morphism,
\Cref{lemma2WRAimpliesAA} implies that $j$ is also an acting
morphism. Let $(l',r')$ be its corresponding derived action
on $X$.

Naturality and injectivity of $\tau$ show that the original
split extension is isomorphic to the pullback of the extension
corresponding to $(l',r')$ along $q$. Hence
\[
\begin{aligned}
l'(\varphi(b),x)&=l'(q(b),x)=l(b,x),\\
r'(x,\varphi(b))&=r'(x,q(b))=r(x,b),
\end{aligned}
\]
for every $b\in B$ and $x\in X$. These equalities determine $(l',r')$ uniquely, since $q$ is surjective.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- definition-14: body_md -->
Let $X$ be an $\mathbb{F}$-vector space. A \emph{bilinear partial operation}
on $X$ is a pair $(D,\mu)$ consisting of a vector subspace
\[
D\leq X\otimes X
\]
and a linear map
\[
\mu\colon D\to X.
\]
Given $x,y\in X$, we say that the product $xy$ is
\emph{defined} if $x\otimes y\in D$. In this case, we set
\[
xy\coloneqq\mu(x\otimes y).
\]
<!-- end definition-14 -->
<!-- definition-15: body_md -->
A \emph{partial algebra} over $\mathbb{F}$ is a triple $(X,D,\mu)$, where
$X$ is an $\mathbb{F}$-vector space and $(D,\mu)$ is a bilinear partial
operation on $X$.

The partial algebra $(X,D,\mu)$ is said to be \emph{total} if $D=X\otimes X$. In this case, $\mu$ is the linearisation of an ordinary bilinear
multiplication on $X$.
<!-- end definition-15 -->
<!-- definition-16: body_md -->
Let $(X,D,\mu)$ and $(X',D',\mu')$ be partial algebras over $\mathbb{F}$.
A \emph{homomorphism of partial algebras} is a linear map
$f\colon X\to X'$ such that
\[
(f\otimes f)(D)\subseteq D' \quad \text{ and } \quad
f\circ\mu=\mu'\circ\left.(f\otimes f)\right|_D.
\]
<!-- end definition-16 -->
<!-- remark-20: body_md -->
\label{remzeroPAlg}
The category $\ensuremath{\mathsf{PAlg}}$ is pointed. Its zero object is the zero vector space endowed with its unique bilinear partial operation, which is total, and we denote it by~$0$, as usual. Indeed, let $(X,D,\mu)$ be a partial algebra. Since $D$ is a vector subspace of $X\otimes X$, it contains the zero tensor, so that the unique linear map from the zero vector space to $X$ is a homomorphism of partial algebras. The unique linear map from $X$ to the zero vector space is clearly a homomorphism of partial algebras as well. Consequently, the zero morphism between two partial algebras is the zero linear map. Moreover, for every $x\in X$, the tensors $x\otimes0$ and $0\otimes x$ are zero and hence belong to~$D$, so that the products $x0$ and $0x$ are always defined and equal to~$0$.
<!-- end remark-20 -->
<!-- definition-17: body_md -->
Let $(X,D,\mu)$ be a partial algebra, and let
\[
t\in\operatorname{FM}(\{x_1,\ldots,x_n\})
\]
be a non-associative word. For every $\mathbf a=(a_1,\ldots,a_n)\in X^n$, we define the \emph{partial evaluation} of $t$ at $\mathbf a$
recursively as follows.

If $t=x_i$, then $t$ is defined at $\mathbf a$, and $t(\mathbf a)=a_i$. 

If $t=t_1t_2$, assume that $t_1$ and $t_2$ are defined at $\mathbf a$, with respective values
\[
t_1(\mathbf a)=b
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
t_2(\mathbf a)=c.
\]
Then $t$ is defined at $\mathbf a$ if and only if $b\otimes c\in D$, and in this case
\[
t(\mathbf a)
=
\mu(b\otimes c).
\]
If one of these conditions is not satisfied, then $t$ is not defined at $\mathbf a$.

Let $p\in\mathbb{F}[\operatorname{FM}(\{x_1,\ldots,x_n\})]$ have the unique expansion
\[
p=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\lambda_jt_j,
\]
where the $t_j$ are pairwise distinct non-associative words and
$\lambda_j\in\mathbb{F}\setminus\{0\}$.
We say that $p$ is \emph{defined} at $\mathbf a$ if every $t_j$ is
defined at $\mathbf a$. In this case, we set
\[
p(\mathbf a)=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\lambda_jt_j(\mathbf a).
\]
We allow $r=0$; thus the zero polynomial is defined at every $\mathbf a\in X^n$ and has value zero.

We say that $(X,D,\mu)$ \emph{satisfies the polynomial identity}
\[
p(x_1,\ldots,x_n)=0
\]
if $p(\mathbf a)=0$ for every $\mathbf a\in X^n$ at which $p$ is defined.
<!-- end definition-17 -->
<!-- example-16: body_md -->
Let $x,y,z\in X$. The monomial $x(yz)$ is defined at
$(x,y,z)$ if and only if
\[
y\otimes z\in D \quad \text{ and } \quad x\otimes\mu(y\otimes z)\in D.
\]
When these conditions hold, its value is
\[
x(yz)
=
\mu\bigl(
x\otimes\mu(y\otimes z)
\bigr).
\]

Similarly, the monomial $(xy)z$ is defined if and only if
\[
x\otimes y\in D \quad \text{ and } \quad \mu(x\otimes y)\otimes z\in D,
\]
and in this case
\[
(xy)z
=
\mu\bigl(
\mu(x\otimes y)\otimes z
\bigr).
\]
<!-- end example-16 -->
<!-- example-17: body_md -->
A partial algebra $(X,D,\mu)$ is \emph{associative} if
\[
x(yz)=(xy)z
\]
for every $x,y,z\in X$ for which both sides are defined.
<!-- end example-17 -->
<!-- remark-21: body_md -->
Let $X$ be a non-associative algebra, and let
\[
\widetilde m_X\colon X\otimes X\to X
\]
be the linearisation of its multiplication. Then
\[
\widetilde U(X)
=
(X,X\otimes X,\widetilde m_X)
\]
is a total algebra. This construction defines a canonical fully faithful functor $\widetilde U\colon\mathcal{V}\to\ensuremath{\mathsf{PAlg}}$ for every variety of non-associative algebras $\mathcal{V}$. In particular, $\widetilde U$ sends the zero algebra to the zero object of~$\ensuremath{\mathsf{PAlg}}$.
<!-- end remark-21 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:morphism-from-total}
Let $B$ be a non-associative algebra, let $(X,D,\mu)$ be a partial
algebra, and let $f\colon B\to X$ be a linear map. Then
\[
f\colon\widetilde U(B)\to(X,D,\mu)
\]
is a homomorphism of partial algebras if and only if, for every
$b,b'\in B$,
\[
f(b)\otimes f(b')\in D
\]
and
\[
f(bb')
=
\mu\bigl(f(b)\otimes f(b')\bigr).
\]
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
The pure tensors $b\otimes b'$ span $B\otimes B$. Therefore,
\[
(f\otimes f)(B\otimes B)\subseteq D
\]
if and only if $f(b)\otimes f(b')\in D$ for all $b,b'\in B$.
Likewise, the equality
\[
f\circ\widetilde m_B
=
\mu\circ(f\otimes f)
\]
between linear maps $B\otimes B\to X$ may be checked on pure tensors.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:partial-from-bilinear-map}
Let $W$ be a vector subspace of a vector space $Z$, and let
\[
m\colon W\times W\to Z
\]
be a bilinear map. Denote its linearisation by $\widetilde m\colon W\otimes W\to Z$. Then
\[
D_m\coloneqq\widetilde m^{-1}(W)
\]
is a vector subspace of $W\otimes W$, and
\[
\mu_m\coloneqq\left.\widetilde m\right|_{D_m}
\colon D_m\to W
\]
defines a partial algebra $(W,D_m,\mu_m)$. For $u,v\in W$, the product
$u\cdot v$ is defined precisely when $m(u,v)\in W$, and in this case
\[
u\cdot v=m(u,v).
\]
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Since $W\leq Z$ and $\widetilde m$ is linear, the inverse image
$D_m=\widetilde m^{-1}(W)$ is a vector subspace of $W\otimes W$.
The remaining assertions follow immediately from the definitions.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{prop_semidirect}\cite{WRAAlg}
Let $X$ and $B$ be objects of $\mathcal{V}$. Given a pair of bilinear maps
\[
l\colon B\times X\to X,
\qquad
r\colon X\times B\to X,
\]
we construct $(B\oplus X,\cdot)$ as in
\eqref{product on semi direct}. Then $(B\oplus X,\cdot)$ is an object
of $\mathcal{V}$ if and only if
\[
\Phi_{k,i}(\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_k)=0
\]
for every $i=1,\ldots,n_k$ and every mixed choice of
$\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_k$, where each $\alpha_j$ is either of the
form $(b,0)$, with $b\in B$, or of the form $(0,x)$, with $x\in X$,
and at least one element of each form occurs.
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\cite{WRAAlg}
Suppose that every identity of $\mathcal{V}$ is a consequence of the
$\lambda/\mu$-rules. Then $(B\oplus X,\cdot)$ is an object of $\mathcal{V}$ if
and only if the following identities hold:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(1)]
$b\ast(xx')
 =\lambda_1(b\ast x)x'+\cdots+\lambda_8x(x'\ast b)$;

\item[(2)]
$(xx')\ast b
 =\mu_1(b\ast x)x'+\cdots+\mu_8x(x'\ast b)$;

\item[(3)]
$x(x'\ast b)
 =\lambda_1(xx')\ast b+\cdots+\lambda_8x'(b\ast x)$;

\item[(4)]
$(x'\ast b)x
 =\mu_1(xx')\ast b+\cdots+\mu_8x'(b\ast x)$;

\item[(5)]
$x(b\ast x')
 =\lambda_1(x\ast b)x'+\cdots+\lambda_8b\ast(x'x)$;

\item[(6)]
$(b\ast x')x
 =\mu_1(x\ast b)x'+\cdots+\mu_8b\ast(x'x)$;

\item[(7)]
$x\ast(bb')
 =\lambda_1(x\ast b)\ast b'
  +\cdots+\lambda_8b\ast(b'\ast x)$;

\item[(8)]
$(bb')\ast x
 =\mu_1(x\ast b)\ast b'
  +\cdots+\mu_8b\ast(b'\ast x)$;

\item[(9)]
$b\ast(b'\ast x)
 =\lambda_1(bb')\ast x+\cdots+\lambda_8b'\ast(x\ast b)$;

\item[(10)]
$(b'\ast x)\ast b
 =\mu_1(bb')\ast x+\cdots+\mu_8b'\ast(x\ast b)$;

\item[(11)]
$b\ast(x\ast b')
 =\lambda_1(b\ast x)\ast b'
  +\cdots+\lambda_8x\ast(b'b)$;

\item[(12)]
$(x\ast b')\ast b
=\mu_1(b\ast x)\ast b'
 +\cdots+\mu_8 x\ast(b'b)$;
 \end{enumerate}
for every $b,b'\in B$ and $x,x'\in X$.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- definition-18: body_md -->
\label{E(X)}\cite{WRAAlg}
Let $X$ be an object of $\mathcal{V}$. We define $\mathcal{E}(X)$ as the subspace of
all pairs
\[
f=(f\ast-,-\ast f)\in\operatorname{End}(X)^2
\]
satisfying
\[
\Phi_{k,i}(\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_k)=0,
\qquad
i=1,\ldots,n_k,
\]
for every $k$, every $j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}$ and all $\alpha_t\in X$ with $t\neq j$, where $\alpha_j=f$. Here, we write
\[
fx\coloneqq f\ast x,
\qquad
xf\coloneqq x\ast f.
\]
We consider the bilinear map
\[
m_X\colon
\mathcal{E}(X)\times\mathcal{E}(X)\to\operatorname{End}(X)^2
\]
defined by
\[
m_X\left(
(f\ast-,-\ast f),(g\ast-,-\ast g)
\right)
=
(h\ast-,-\ast h),
\]
where
\begin{align*}
x\ast h
={}&\lambda_1(x\ast f)\ast g
  +\lambda_2(f\ast x)\ast g
  +\lambda_3g\ast(x\ast f)
  +\lambda_4g\ast(f\ast x)\\
 &+\lambda_5(x\ast g)\ast f
  +\lambda_6(g\ast x)\ast f
  +\lambda_7f\ast(x\ast g)
  +\lambda_8f\ast(g\ast x)
\intertext{and}
h\ast x
={}&\mu_1(x\ast f)\ast g
  +\mu_2(f\ast x)\ast g
  +\mu_3g\ast(x\ast f)
  +\mu_4g\ast(f\ast x)\\
 &+\mu_5(x\ast g)\ast f
  +\mu_6(g\ast x)\ast f
  +\mu_7f\ast(x\ast g)
  +\mu_8f\ast(g\ast x).
\end{align*}
<!-- end definition-18 -->
<!-- remark-22: body_md -->
The subspace $\mathcal{E}(X)$ does not depend on the chosen defining family of identities: since the multilinear identities of $\mathcal{V}$ are linear combinations of the~$\Phi_{k,i}$ evaluated at monomials and multiplied by further monomials, every multilinear identity of $\mathcal{V}$ vanishes when exactly one of its variables is replaced by $f\in\mathcal{E}(X)$ and the others by elements of~$X$.
<!-- end remark-22 -->
<!-- remark-23: body_md -->
Suppose that every identity of $\mathcal{V}$ is a consequence of the
$\lambda/\mu$-rules. Then $\mathcal{E}(X)$ is the subspace of all pairs
$(f\ast-,-\ast f)\in\operatorname{End}(X)^2$ satisfying
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(1)]
$f\ast(xx')
 =\lambda_1(f\ast x)x'+\cdots+\lambda_8x(x'\ast f)$;

\item[(2)]
$(xx')\ast f
 =\mu_1(f\ast x)x'+\cdots+\mu_8x(x'\ast f)$;

\item[(3)]
$x(x'\ast f)
 =\lambda_1(xx')\ast f+\cdots+\lambda_8x'(f\ast x)$;

\item[(4)]
$(x'\ast f)x
 =\mu_1(xx')\ast f+\cdots+\mu_8x'(f\ast x)$;

\item[(5)]
$x(f\ast x')
 =\lambda_1(x\ast f)x'+\cdots+\lambda_8f\ast(x'x)$;

\item[(6)]
$(f\ast x')x
 =\mu_1(x\ast f)x'+\cdots+\mu_8f\ast(x'x)$;
\end{enumerate}
for any $x,x'\in X$.
<!-- end remark-23 -->
<!-- remark-24: body_md -->
The choice of the $\lambda/\mu$-rules does not affect the underlying
vector space of $\mathcal{E}(X)$, but it plays an essential role in the
definition of the bilinear map $m_X$ and hence in the partial
algebra structure on $\mathcal{E}(X)$.
<!-- end remark-24 -->
<!-- remark-25: body_md -->
In general, the bilinear map
\[
m_X\colon\mathcal{E}(X)\times\mathcal{E}(X)\to\operatorname{End}(X)^2
\]
does not corestrict to $\mathcal{E}(X)$, i.e., it does not define a bilinear operation on $\mathcal{E}(X)$. In other words, there may exist
$f,g\in\mathcal{E}(X)$ such that
\[
m_X(f,g)\notin\mathcal{E}(X).
\]
Even when $m_X$ corestricts to a bilinear operation on
$\mathcal{E}(X)$, the resulting total algebra may fail to satisfy
some of the defining identities of $\mathcal{V}$.
<!-- end remark-25 -->
<!-- example-18: body_md -->
\label{Example associative}
Let $\mathcal{V}=\ensuremath{\mathsf{Assoc}}$, and let $X$ be an associative algebra. Then $\mathcal{E}(X)\cong\operatorname{Bim}(X)$ as vector spaces, where $\operatorname{Bim}(X)$ is the associative algebra of bimultipliers of $X$ described in~\Cref{exassoc}. If we consider the standard choice of the $\lambda/\mu$-rules given by
\[
\lambda_1=\mu_8=1
\]
and
\[
\lambda_i=\mu_j=0
\]
for every $i\in\{2,\ldots,8\}$ and $j\in\{1,\ldots,7\}$, then the above isomorphism is also an isomorphism of associative algebras.
<!-- end example-18 -->
<!-- example-19: body_md -->
\label{exLeib}
Let $\mathcal{V}=\ensuremath{\mathsf{Leib}}$, and let $X$ be a Leibniz algebra. Then
\[
\mathcal{E}(X)
=
\left\{
(f\ast-,-\ast f)\in\operatorname{End}(X)^2
\,\middle|\,
\begin{aligned}
f\ast[x,y]
  &= [f\ast x,y]-[f\ast y,x],\\
[x,y]\ast f
  &= [x\ast f,y]+[x,y\ast f],\\
[x,f\ast y]&+[x,y\ast f]
  =0
\end{aligned}
\, \forall x,y\in X
\right\}.
\]
With the choice of the $\lambda/\mu$-rules as in \Cref{esLeib}, we have that $\mathcal{E}(X)$ is isomorphic to the Leibniz algebra
\[
\operatorname{Bider}(X)
=
\left\{
(d,D)\in\operatorname{End}(X)^2
\,\middle|\,
\begin{aligned}
d([x,y])
  &= [d(x),y]+[x,d(y)],\\
D([x,y])
  &= [D(x),y]-[D(y),x],\\
[x,d(y)]
  &= [x,D(y)]
\end{aligned}
\quad \forall x,y\in X
\right\}
\]
of \emph{biderivations} of $X$~\cite{loday}, whose bracket is given by
\[
\left[(d,D),(d',D')\right]
=
\bigl(
d\circ d'-d'\circ d,\,
D\circ d'-d'\circ D
\bigr).
\]
The isomorphism $\operatorname{Bider}(X) \to \mathcal{E}(X)$ is given by the linear map
\[
(d,D) \mapsto (D,-d).
\]
On the other hand, if we choose the $\lambda/\mu$-rules as
\begin{align*}
[x,[y,z]]
  &= [[x,y],z]-[[x,z],y],\\
[[y,z],x]
  &= [[y,x],z]+[y,[z,x]],
\end{align*}
the underlying vector space of $\mathcal{E}(X)$, identified with $\operatorname{Bider}(X)$ via the above isomorphism, is endowed with the non-associative operation defined in~\cite[Definition~5.2]{Casas}:
\[
\bigl\{(d,D),(d',D')\bigr\}
=
\bigl(
d\circ d'-d'\circ d,\,
D\circ D'-d'\circ D
\bigr).
\]
This operation does not, in general, satisfy the right Leibniz
identity and therefore does not define a Leibniz algebra structure on
$\mathcal{E}(X)$. For instance, if $X=\mathbb{F}$ is the one-dimensional abelian algebra, then $\operatorname{Bider}(X) \cong \mathbb{F}^2$, and $u=(0,1)$ satisfies $\{u,u\}=u$, so that $\{u,\{u,u\}\}=u\neq0$, whereas $[x,[y,y]]=0$ for all elements $x,y$ of a right Leibniz algebra.
<!-- end example-19 -->
<!-- example-20: body_md -->
\label{exnil2}
Let $\mathcal{V}=\ensuremath{\mathsf{Nil}}_2(\ensuremath{\mathsf{Alg}})$ be the variety of $2$-nilpotent algebras
(see~\cite{LaRosaMancini1,LaRosaMancini2}), determined
by the identities
\[
x(yz)=(yz)x=0.
\]
Then
\[
\mathcal{E}(X)
=
\left\{
(f\ast-,-\ast f)\in\operatorname{End}(X)^2
\,\middle|\,
\begin{aligned}
f\ast(xy)&=(xy)\ast f=0,\\
(f\ast x)y&=x(y\ast f)=0,\\
x(f \ast y)&=(x \ast f)y=0,
\end{aligned}
\quad \forall x,y\in X
\right\},
\]
and, choosing $\lambda_i=\mu_i=0$ for $i=1,\ldots,8$, the bilinear map $m_X$ is given by
\[
m_X\left((f\ast-,-\ast f),(g\ast-,-\ast g)\right)=(0,0).
\]
Thus, $\mathcal{E}(X)$ is an abelian algebra.
<!-- end example-20 -->
<!-- example-21: body_md -->
\label{Example Nilk}
Let $\mathcal{V}=\ensuremath{\mathsf{Nil}}_k(\ensuremath{\mathsf{Assoc}})$, with $k\geq3$. Then
\[
\mathcal{E}(X)
=
\left\{
(f\ast-,-\ast f)\in\operatorname{Bim}(X)
\;\middle|\;
\begin{aligned}
&f\ast(x_1\cdots x_k)=0=(x_1\cdots x_k)\ast f\\
&(x_1\cdots x_j)\bigl(f\ast(x_{j+1}\cdots x_k)\bigr)=0
\end{aligned}\right\}
\]
